Foreword

The global environmental landscape is evolving rapidly, demanding urgent collective action to address
climate change, pollution, and resource depletion. With global plastic production exceeding 400 million
tonnes annually, over 60% ends up in landfills or the natural environment. Under Hon'ble Prime Minister
Shri Narendra Modi's visionary 'Life' Movement-Lifestyle for Environment-India has emerged as a global
leader in sustainability and climate resilience. Gujarat aligns deeply with this vision, demonstrating that
economic growth and environmental stewardship can go handin hand.

Under Hon'ble Chief Minister Shri Bhupendrabhai Patel, Gujarat has advanced environmental
governance, integrating circular economy principles and making cities cleaner and greener. The state has
operationalised, strengthened source segregation, promoted Extended Producer Responsibility
frameworks, and implemented the National Clean Air Programme with interventions like urban
greening, e-mobility and real-time pollution monitoring.

The Gujarat Pollution Control Board (GPCB) has consistently demonstrated its commitment to
safeguarding the environment through effective monitoring, regulation, and promotion of sustainable
practices across the state. Established with the vision to ensure a cleaner and healthier Gujarat, GPCB
continues to enforce environmental laws, support industries in adopting eco-friendly technologies, and
engage stakeholders in environmental awareness initiatives. The Board remains focused on enhancing
the quality of air, water and land resources, thereby contributing to the overall well-being of the people of
Gujarat. This Annual Report highlights GPCB's key achievements, ongoing initiatives and future
strategies aimed at fostering responsible environmental stewardship.

Beyond pollution control, Gujarat has planted millions of saplings, restored mangroves and wetlands,
supported eco-sensitive zones and promoted cleaner production technologies. The state is converging
efforts across departments to drive sustainable consumption, green jobs and low-carbon transitions,
with GPCB playing a pivotal technical and regulatory role.

Looking ahead, Gujarat envisions clean air, safe water, efficient waste management and ecological
balance as everyday realities. We aim to scale waste-to-wealth initiatives, establish plastic circularity
ecosystems, strengthen community air quality planning, integrate geospatial decision-making tools and
promote youth-led green innovations, positioning Gujarat as a national model of sustainable
industrialisation rooted in environmental justice and intergenerational equity.

| extend best wishes to Team Gujarat Pollution Control Board for their unwavering commitment to
environmental transformation, professionalism, dedication and vision. This Annual Report is a
testament to Gujarat's collaborative journey towards a sustainable, circular and resilient future.

(Sanjeev Kumar)

Preface

Itis with sense of pride and responsibility that | present the Annual Report of the Gujarat Pollution Control
Board (GPCB) for 2024-25. This report highlights the Board's ongoing efforts, progressive initiatives and
regulatory actions for prevention and control of pollution and promoting sustainable development
across Gujarat.

Since its establishment in 1974 under the Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, GPCB has
evolved into one of India's foremost State Pollution Control Board. With Gujarat as a major industrial hub,
the Board continues to address emerging environmental challenges while aligning with national policies
and global commitments like the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). GPCB plays its role not merely
as regulator but also as facilitator.

This year, the common environmental infrastructure in Gujarat has received a boost through the
operationalization of 45 Common Effluent Treatment Plants (CETPs), expansion of Multiple Effect
Evaporators (MEEs), and enhanced waste treatment facilities, including operational common TSDFs and
captive TSDFs for industrial hazardous waste disposal. These efforts support small and medium
industries and demonstrate Gujarat's cooperative model in pollution management. The expansion of
Common Spray Dryers, Hazardous Waste Incineration Facilities, pre-processing facilities, and recycling
units further consolidated Gujarat's environmental infrastructure, positioning it as a national model for
waste management.

Environmental monitoring has remained a key priority, with programs covering ambient air, surface
water, groundwater, noise, and coastal quality. Through its robust State and National Air Quality
Monitoring Programmes, Water Quality Monitoring under GEMS and MINARS projects, festival
monitoring initiatives, and coastal surveillance, GPCB has ensured vigilant oversight of environmental
health across Gujarat's diverse ecosystems.

During the year, the Board convened four Board meetings and several committee meetings, taking major
decisions on recruitment, financial allocations, environmental guidelines, and legal matters.
Committees such as Technical, Scientific, Purchase, Norms, and Expert Committees have functioned
effectively, ensuring decisions are technically robust and administratively sound. Recruitment rules were
approved for key posts including Assistant Environmental Engineers and Scientific Assistants, alongside
approval of guidelines for ready-mix concrete plants and environmental compensations.

GPCB's efforts in public awareness deserve special mention. Campaigns during festivals, Swachhta
campaigns, and programmes for plastic waste reduction have engaged communities, students and
industries alike. The Board has effectively promoted the 3Rs (Reduce, Reuse, Recycle), encouraging
citizens to adopt environmentally responsible lifestyles in alignment with Mission LiFE (Lifestyle for
Environment) and national sustainability goals.

This report reflects our commitment to continuous improvement and transparent governance in
safeguarding Gujarat's environment.

(R. B. Barad)
Gujarat Pollution Control Board
“Paryavaran Bhavan”, Sector-10A,
Gandhinagar- 382 010, Gujarat

D.M. Thaker
Member Secretary

Acknowledgement

It gives me immense pleasure to present the Annual Report of the Gujarat Pollution Control Board (GPCB)
for the year 2024-25. This report is a reflection of our sustained efforts towards strengthening
environmental governance and promoting sustainable development across the state of Gujarat.

| would like to express my sincere gratitude to all stakeholders—including government departments,
industries, academic institutions, NGOs, and the citizens of Gujarat—for their active participation,
cooperation, and continued commitment to environmental protection. The achievements documented
in this report are the result of collective efforts toward ensuring a cleaner, greener, and more resilient
Gujarat.

This report encapsulates the activities and initiatives undertaken by GPCB during the year, highlighting
key developments in the areas of water and air quality management, waste management, consent
management, etc. It also captures our efforts in driving transparency through digital platforms, citizen
engagement, and data-driven environmental decision-making.

We remain committed to our mission of environmental protection and look forward to continued
collaboration with all stakeholders as we stride ahead in our journey towards sustainable environmental
stewardship.

(D.M. Thaker)

CHAPTER: 1!
Introduction

1.1 Introduction

The Gujarat Pollution Control Board (GPCB) was
established on 15th October 1974 under the provisions of
the Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1974.
As a leading industrial state, Gujarat required effective
measures for pollution control and environmental
protection. The Board is responsible for implementing
various pollution controllaws and regulations.

Initially, in October 1974, the Board commenced
operations with a staff of 25 employees. As of 31st March
2025, the sanctioned staff strength stands at 866, with 304
employees currently appointed. The recruitment process
for an additional 316 posts is underway.

Currently, the Board operates 27 regional offices along with
the Head Office across the state. A list of regional offices and
their addresses is provided in Annexure-1. Of these, eight
regional offices — Vapi, Surat, Bharuch, Vadodara, Rajkot,
Bhavnagar, Jamnagar, and Kutch (West) — are equipped
with laboratory facilities. The Head Office and Central
Laboratory are located in Gandhinagar.

The Central Laboratory undertakes sample analysis for
several districts, including Anmedabad (urban and rural),
Gandhinagar, Mehsana, Palanpur, and Himatnagar. It also
plays a key role in developing analytical methods for water,
wastewater, air, and hazardous waste samples.

1.2. Functions

The primary function of the Board is to enforce the
provisions of the following Acts, Rules, Notifications, and
Directives, as issued by the competent authorities from
timetotime:

+ The Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act,
1974

+ The Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Cess
Act, 1977

+  TheAir(Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1981

+ The Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, along with the
relevant rules notified thereunder:

+ The Hazardous and Other Wastes (Management
and Transboundary Movement) Rules, 2016

+ The Bio-Medical Waste Management Rules, 2016
+ The Plastic Waste Management Rules, 2016

+ The Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016

+ The E-Waste (Management) Rules, 2022

+ The Construction and Demolition Waste
Management Rules, 2016

+ The Manufacture, Storage and Import of

Hazardous Chemicals Rules, 1989

+ The Noise Pollution (Regulation and Control)
Rules, 2000

+ The Ozone Depleting Substances (Regulation and
Control) Rules, 2000

+ The Battery Waste Management Rules, 2022

«The Environmental Impact Assessment Notification,
2006

+ TheCoastal Regulation Zone (CRZ) Notification, 2011
* The Environment Audit Scheme, 1996
+ The Right to Information Act, 2005

+ The Gujarat (Right of Citizens to Public Services) Act,
2013

+  TheFlyAsh Utilisation Notification, 2016

+ Compliance with directives issued by the courts

In accordance with the provisions of the aforementioned

Acts and Rules, the Board undertakes the following

activities:

* Prevention, control, and abatement of pollution across
the State, along with efforts to improve the overall
environmental quality.

+ Advising the State Government on matters related to
the prevention and control of pollution in water, air, and
land.

+ Approving the appropriate location of new industries
froma pollution control perspective.

+ Promoting the reuse and recycling of sewage and
industrial effluents.

+ Monitoring the quality of water bodies such as rivers,
wells, and ponds across the State.

* Conducting air quality monitoring in industrial areas of
major cities within the State.

+ Collecting and compiling technical and statistical data
related to water and air pollution.

+ Setting standards for the discharge of liquid effluents
and gaseous emissions.

+ Developing methods for the treatment of effluents and
air pollutants using various pollution control
technologies.

+ Identifying suitable sites for the disposal of hazardous
wastes.

+ Organizing training programs, seminars, and
workshops on pollution control.

+ Raising public awareness on environmental pollution
prevention and control.
Promoting the cleanliness and maintenance of wells
and streams in different regions of the State.

Issuing directives to polluting units to ensure
compliance with pollution control measures

1.3. Objectives

The primary objectives of the Board are pollution control,
prevention, and the protection of environmental quality.
These objectives are outlined as follows:

To bring about comprehensive improvement in the
environmental quality of the State through effective
implementation of environmental laws.

To control pollution at its source to the greatest extent
possible, taking into account technological
advancements, economic feasibility, and the sensitivity
of the receiving environment. This is achieved by
establishing standards for effluent discharge and
gaseous emissions.

To identify suitable sites and develop procedures and
methods for the safe disposal of hazardous wastes.

To maximize the reuse and recycling of sewage and
industrial effluents—particularly for irrigation and
industrial purposes—after appropriate treatment,
thereby conserving water resources and reducing
pollution by minimizing waste discharge into water
bodies.

To minimize the adverse effects of pollution by
selecting appropriate locations for the establishment
of new industrial projects.

To coordinate with other State Government agencies
and local authorities to promote the development of
common environmental infrastructure, such as
Common Effluent Treatment Plants (CETPs),
Treatment, Storage, and Disposal Facilities (TSDFs),
Common Hazardous Waste Incineration Facilities
(CHWIFs), and Common Bio-Medical Waste Treatment
Facilities (CBWTFs).

To maintain close coordination and active engagement
with educational institutions, non-governmental
organizations (NGOs), industry associations, and
government bodies to raise environmental awareness.

1.4 ‘Priorities

The following areas and activities have been identified as
deserving priority attention:

Identifying locations in the State with severe pollution
issues and preparing time-bound action plans for
effective control and mitigation.

GUJARAT POLLUTION

CONTROL BOARD
GANDHINAGAR

Promoting the development of environmental
infrastructure such as CETPs, TSDFs, CHWIFs and
CBWTFs.

Identifying suitable sites for the environmentally safe
disposal of hazardous wastes.

Identifying major air-polluting industries and enforcing
the provisions of the Air Act - 1981 for controlling air
emissions.

Ensuring the proper selection of sites for new industrial
projects, keeping environmental considerations in
view.

Establishing common facilities for the collection,
treatment, and safe disposal of effluents in industrial
estates.

Identifying, developing, and adopting cleaner
production technologies to reduce pollution at the
source.

Prohibiting the discharge of effluents into streams and
other water bodies, while promoting the recycling and
reuse of treated effluents for irrigation and green belt
development.

Promoting the co-processing of waste in cement and
other industries as an environmentally sound
alternative to conventional disposal.

Supporting environmentally sound management
practices for municipal solid waste and municipal
sewage.
Strengthening all-round capacity building in the field of
environmental management across the State of
Gujarat.

Constitution of the Board

GPCB has established Environmental Laboratories at Gandhinagar, along with eight Regional Offices located at Vapi, Surat,
Bharuch, Vadodara, Bhavnagar, Rajkot, Jamnagar, and Kutch (West). These laboratories have been officially notified as State
Laboratories in the Gazette of the Government of Gujarat (GoG).

In addition, the State-of-the-Art Research Laboratory (SARL) at Gandhinagar, equipped with Sophisticated Analytical
Instruments, is recognized under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986.

CHAPTER: IV

Committee of the Board and their Working

4.1 Selection Committees and Promotion
committees

The Board has constituted four Personnel Committees:

+ Two Selection Committees (for Class 1-2 and
Class 3-4)

+ Two Promotion Committees (for Class 1-2 and
Class 3-4)

These committees manage recruitment, selection, and
promotions. Details of committee members are provided in
Annexure-3 (Selection) and Annexure-4 (Promotion).
During 2024-25, one meeting each of the promotion
committee was conducted for promotions of Class 1-2 and
Class 3-4 employees.

4.2 Technical Committee: -

A Technical Committee has been constituted to provide
technical advice and support to the Board on specific
technical issues. Details of committee members is provided
in Annexure-5. During 2024-25, two meetings of the
committee were held.

E28
Eto)

4.3 Purchase Committee: -

A Purchase Committee has been constituted to handle
procurement.

+ For purchases between 21 lakh and 25 lakh: 5
meetings were held, and 56 agendas were approved.

+ For purchases between %5 lakh and 21 crore: 4
meetings were held, and 37 agendas were approved.

+ Forpurchases above 21 crore: 4 meetings were held,
and 11 agendas were approved.

Details of committee members is provided in
Annexure-6.

4.4 Scientific Committee: -

A Scientific Committee has been constituted to streamline
the procurement of scientific instruments, equipment,
glassware, chemicals, filter papers, thimbles, and other
laboratory supplies for the Central and Regional
Laboratories and Regional Offices. The committee also
determines specifications to standardize procurement.
Details of committee members is provided in Annexure -7.

4.4.1 Committee for Modernization and
Strengthening of laboratory: -
A Modernization and Strengthening of laboratory
Committee has been constituted :
+ To provide technical guidance for procuring
scientific instruments

+ To plan expansion and strengthening of GPCB
laboratories for the next five years

+ To guide maintenance and disposal of old
instruments

The first meeting was held on 04.02.2025, recommending
the procurement of 246 sophisticated instruments for
upcoming lab upgrades. Details of committee members is
provided inAnnexure-8.

4.7 Norms Committee: -

The MoEF&CC specifies disposal norms for effluents and
emissions, but SPCBs can impose stricter standards. GPCB
constituted a Norms Committee to decide such cases.
Details of committee members is provided in Annexure -

Environment Audit Committee to oversee Environment
Auditor recognition under the scheme. Details of committee
members is provided in Annexure -12.

handling. GPCB formed this expert committee to review

of committee members is provided in Annexure -14.

5.1 Monitoring andissue of consent to the Industries

5.1.1 Consolidated Consent and Authorization (CCA)

Activities related to the Consolidated Consent and Authorisation (CCA) under the Water Act, 1974; the Air Act, 1981; the
Hazardous and Other Wastes (Management and Transboundary Movement) Rules, 2016; the Bio-Medical Waste Management
Rules, 2016; the Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016; the Plastic Waste Management Rules, 2016; etc., hereinafter referred to
as the Water Act, Air Act, Hazardous Waste Rules, BMW Rules, etc.

Processing and finalisation of Consolidated Consent and Authorisation applications under the Water Act and / the Air
Act and /the Hazardous Waste Rules.

The acceptance, processing, and finalization of Consolidated Consent and Authorisation (CCA) applications and the issuance
of corresponding CCA orders under the Water Act and / the Air Act and / the Hazardous Waste (HW) Rules has been
implemented with effect from April 2003.

As part of e-Governance initiative, GPCB launched the eXtended Green Node (XGN) software on 5th June 2008. This platform
enables stakeholders to submit their applications online, streamlining the entire process.

(A) Finalization of CCAApplications under the Water Act, 1974

One of the core functions of the Board is to process and finalize consent applications submitted by industrial units with

As per the provisions of Section 25, read with Section 26 of the Water Act, every water polluting unit is required to obtain
consent to operate from the Board before bringing into use any outlet for the discharge of sewage or trade effluent into a
stream oron-land.

5.1.3. Authorisation under the Bio-Medical Waste Management Rules, 2016

As per the Bio-Medical Waste Management Rules, 2016, notified under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, the occupier of
any institution generating bio-medical waste including hospitals, nursing homes, clinics, dispensaries, veterinary
institutions, animal houses, pathological laboratories, and blood banks as well as operators of facilities for the collection,
reception, storage, transport, treatment, or disposal of such waste, must apply in Form | and obtain authorisation from the
Board within the notified time schedule.

5.1.4 Authorisation under the Plastics Waste Management Rules 2016

The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEF&CC) published the Plastic Waste Management Rules, 2016
vide Notification No. S.0. 320(E) dated 18th March 2016. These rules have been subsequently amended through the following
notifications:

+  GSRNo.285(E) dated 17th March 2018
+  GSRNo.571(E) dated 12th August 2021
+ GSRNo.133(E) dated 16th February 2022
Key highlights of the Plastic Waste Management Rules are as follows:

+ — Responsibility for plastic waste management has been clearly defined for various stakeholders, including local bodies,
Gram Panchayats, waste generators, producers, importers, and brand owners.

+ The Polluter Pays Principle has been incorporated through the introduction of Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR),
requiring brand owners, producers, and importers to ensure the collection of the plastic waste generated from their
products.

+ Producers, importers, brand owners, recyclers, and reprocessors must apply for registration through the CPCB portal:
https://www.cpcbeprplastic.in/

+ As per Rule 4(1) of the Plastic Waste Management (Amendment) Rules, 2021, carry bags made from virgin or recycled
plastic must not be less than 75 microns in thickness with effect from 30th September 2021, and 120 microns in thickness
with effect from 31st December 2022.

+ According to Rule 4(2) of the Plastic Waste Management (Amendment) Rules, 2021, the manufacture, import, stocking,
distribution, sale, and use of the following single-use plastic (SUP) items, including polystyrene and expanded
polystyrene, are prohibited with effect from 1st July 2022:

(A) Earbuds with plastic sticks, plastic sticks for balloons, plastic flags, candy sticks, ice-cream sticks, polystyrene
(thermocol) for decoration

5.1.6 Environmental Management Systems in the State

Installation of Effluent Treatment Plants

During the year 2024-25, 598 new industrial units have installed effluent treatment plants (ETPs). These include the
installation and commissioning of ETPs by various industries such as thermal power plants, fertilizer units, bulk drug
manufacturers, oil refineries, petrochemical industries, pulp and paper mills, and dye and dye intermediate units.

In addition to new installations, several industries have also upgraded and modified their existing ETPs. As of 31.03.2025, a
total of 14,025 ETPs have been installed and commissioned.

Furthermore, a few industries located in groups or clusters have also initiated activities for the development of common
effluent treatment plants (CETPs).

Installation of Air Pollution Control Equipment

As part of the actions taken by the Board for the implementation of the Air Act, directives in the form of consent conditions
were issued to various industrial units.

During the year 2024-25, a total of 1,021 new industrial units has installed air pollution control equipment to control the
emission of pollutants such as Suspended Particulate Matter, Sulphur Dioxide, Ammonia, Chlorine, Hydrogen Chloride,
Hydrogen Sulphide, etc.

These installations cover various industries, including thermal power plants, cement, fertilizers, bulk drugs, oil refineries,
petrochemicals, pulp and paper, dyes, and dye intermediates.

As of 31.03.2025, a total of 19,840 industrial units have installed air pollution control equipment.

Activities of the Board

5.2.1 Liquid waste management

5.2.1.1 Common Effluent Treatment Plants (CETPs) in Gujarat

A Common Effluent Treatment Plant (CETP) treats industrial effluents through a collective approach, primarily catering to
clusters of small-scale industrial units, making treatment more cost-effective. The wastewater from individual industries often
contains high concentrations of pollutants, and treating it individually to meet the required standards can be both technically
and economically challenging. In such cases, CETPs offer a more practical and economical solution. To address the issue of
inadequate wastewater treatment, the concept of CETPs was introduced in the early 1990s.

Gujarat, a leading state in industrial development, currently has a total hydraulic capacity of approximately 903.545 MLD
across 45 operational CETPs, serving around 7,171 individual industrial units.

GPCB has received details from CETP operators as below:
+ Outof45 existing CETPs, 14 existing CETPs require capacity augmentation.

* 16newCETPsare currently under installation.

These combined efforts are expected to add approximately 612.32 MLD of additional treatment capacity to the state's existing
capacity of 903.545 MLD.

5.2.1.2

The Multiple Effect Evaporator (MEE), widely used in
industrial process plants, has emerged in recent years as a
critical unit in effluent treatment systems. Gujarat has
embraced this concept extensively, with industries now
segregating the concentrated stream of their effluents for
treatment through MEEs. This approach has significantly
reduced the load on conventional effluent treatment
plants. More importantly, it has enhanced the effectiveness
of biological treatment processes, resulting in substantial
reductions in key pollution parameters such as BOD, COD,
and Ammoniacal Nitrogen.

Pre-treatment of concentrated effluents using strippers to
remove volatile organics, followed by concentration in
MEEs and moisture reduction through technologies such as
ATFD (Agitated Thin Film Dryer), has yielded excellent
results particularly in dye intermediates, bulk drug
intermediates, and pesticide intermediate manufacturing
units.

GUJARAT POLLUTION

CONTROL BOARD
GANDHINAGAR

Multiple Effect Evaporators (MEEs) Common and Captive

Gujarat, known for its strong cooperative industrial base,
has made significant progress by adopting this approach
through a cooperative model, establishing Common
Environmental Infrastructure at the industrial cluster level.

A significant number of captive MEEs have been installed
across the state, particularly in the districts ie. Bharuch:
153, Ahmedabad: 58, Vadodara: 44, Valsad: 40, Kutch: 26,
Anand: 21, Surat: 20, Mehsana: 20, Morbi: 12. The total
number of MEEs in Gujarat stands at 455, with additional
installations in smaller numbers across other districts.

Gujarat continues to hold a leading position in India's
environmental sector, thanks to the development of
integrated environmental infrastructure within its
industrial estates.

Gujarat was the first state in India to proactively address the issue of hazardous waste management by introducing the
innovative concept of a Common Treatment, Storage, and Disposal Facility (TSDF) for clusters of industries. Establishing a
TSDF for individual industries is often unviable due to the relatively small quantities of hazardous waste generated by each
unit.

Gujarat leads the country in the development of TSDF sites. It is the first state to establish 25 common TSDF sites, which
include:

+ 13 operational sites
*  3closed and capped sites
+ 1non-operational site

*  8proposed sites

In addition, Gujarat has 14 captive TSDF sites operated by individual industries. As of now, 11 captive TSDFs are operational,
and 3areclosed.

like mother liquors, and toxic effluents require thermal

technologies in Gujarat, the demand for new incineration
facilities has not increased proportionally with the growth
inthe number of industries.

5.2.2.3 Common Facilities for Decontamination of Discarded drums/ barrels/ Containers

In Gujarat, a total of 127 common facilities for the decontamination of discarded barrels and containers have been authorized
under Rule 9 of the Hazardous and Other Wastes (Management and Transboundary Movement) Rules, 2016.

Details of Common Decontamination facilities in Gujarat are as under: (As on March 2025)

GPCB promotes the concept of Community Boilers or Common Boilers to reduce pollution from small and medium-scale
industries. Instead of each unit operating its own boiler, multiple industries are encouraged to share a centralized boiler
system. This approach improves fuel efficiency, reduces air emissions, and simplifies compliance with environmental
regulations.

Common boilers are typically operated and maintained by third-party operators equipped with proper emission control
technologies such as Electrostatic Precipitators (ESPs) or bag filters. This ensures uniform monitoring and minimizes the risk
of non-compliance. The model is especially effective within industrial estates and supports the transition to cleaner fuels like
PNG (piped natural gas) or biomass.

Overall, this initiative promotes environmental sustainability, enhances ease of doing business, and improves air quality
which creates a win-win situation forthe economy, environment, and industry.

CHAPTER: VI

6.1 Sewage Management

GPCB plays a pivotal role in ensuring environmental compliance and promoting sustainable wastewater management across
the state. Through effective regulation, coordination, and capacity building, GPCB facilitates the development of robust
sewage treatment infrastructure aligned with Gujarat's vision for sustainable urban growth and environmental protection.

In Gujarat, the installation and operation of Sewage Treatment Plants (STPs) within Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) are primarily
led by the Gujarat Urban Development Corporation (GUDC), the state's designated nodal agency. Under initiatives such as
AMRUT and SJMMSVY, GUDC undertakes various urban infrastructure projects, including the construction of STPs.

These initiatives aim to enhance urban sanitation, reduce pollution in natural water bodies, and promote sustainable
development. GPCB complements these efforts by monitoring compliance, providing technical guidance, and strictly
enforcing environmental standards across all urban infrastructure projects.

Status of Sewage Treatment Infrastructure in Gujarat
(As on March 2025)

Category Number of STPs Total Capacity (MLD)
Existing STPs (Total) 209 6104.90

- Operational 193

- Under trial run / temporarily non-operational 16

Under Installation 52 1449.13

Under Planning 34 1617.71

Estimated Sewage Generation = 4414.00

These developments underscore Gujarat's ongoing commitment to expanding and upgrading its sewage treatment capacity
through collaborative efforts by municipal bodies and GUDC.

In addition to public infrastructure, several private industrial and residential townships in Gujarat have established their own
STPs to manage sewage at the source. By treating wastewater before discharge or reuse, these plants significantly reduce
environmental impact. GPCB regulates and monitors these private STPs to ensure compliance with effluent discharge
standards and to maintain operational efficiency.

Together, these public and private initiatives are driving substantial progress toward a cleaner and more sustainable future for
the state.

6.2. Municipal Solid Waste Management

The Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016, effective from 08th April, 2016, require all municipal authorities to collect,
segregate, transport, and properly dispose of solid waste in towns and cities. Authorization from GPCB is mandatory for setting
up waste processing and disposal facilities, including landfills.

In Gujarat, 156 Nagarpalikas, 8 Municipal Corporations, 4 Notified Areas, and 1 Urban Development Authority are designated
for compliance. The state generates around 12,000 MT of solid waste daily. Currently, 70 Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) have valid
authorizations, while 31 ULBs generating less than 5 MT/day are exempt. GPCB regularly monitors progress, engages district
officials, and has issued 8 Notices of Direction and 22 Show Cause Notices for violations during 2024-25.

To manage waste effectively, Gujarat has adopted common secured landfills, with GUDC as the nodal agency. Based on
detailed studies, 29 regional landfill clusters are being developed for municipalities within a 50 km radius. Out of 8 Municipal
Corporations, Surat, Rajkot, Vadodara, and Ahmedabad having operational individual land fill site. Gandhinagar Municipal
Corporation using land fill site of Anmedabad Municipal Corporation, while Land fill site of Jamnagar, Bhavnagar and
Junagadh Municipal Corporation are under development. GPCB also encourages smaller towns to use nearby municipal
landfill sites for non-compostable municipal waste disposal.

Municipalities must submit annual compliance reports by 30th June, and GPCB conducts periodic inspections, issuing notices
for any non-compliance to ensure proper statewide implementation of solid waste management practices.

Waste Generation and Collection

+  All164 ULBs in Gujarat generate approximately 12,000
metric tonnes of waste daily (8,824.88 MT from
Municipalities and 3,175.12 MT from Corporations).

+ Waste is collected door-to-door and transported to
Material Recovery Facilities (MRFs) for further sorting
and management.

Composting Plants

+ The state has 597 composting plants
(vermicomposting, aerobic, and anaerobic) with a
combined capacity of 6,157 MT/day.

Biogas Plants

+ — Atotal of 35 biogas plants operate in Gujarat, handling
163.3 MT/day of waste.

Material Recovery Facilities (MRFs)

+ There are 204 MRF plants in the state with a processing
capacity of 4,262.3 MT/day.

Refuse Derived Fuel (RDF) Plants

+ The state has 5 RDF plants, with a combined capacity
of 1,055 TPD (tonnes per day).

Waste-to-Energy (WTE) Plants

+ 2 operational plants located in Ahmedabad and

Jamnagar, using 1,600 TPD of waste to generate 15 MW
and 7.5 MW ofelectricity, respectively.

* The Ahmedabad plant incinerates municipal solid
waste using RDF-based technology, producing 65 TPH
steam that generates 15 MW/hour of electricity,
employing over 250 local workers.

+ Additionally, 4 new WTE plants are under
development with a combined capacity of 59.7 MW,
utilizing 6,100 TPD of solid waste.

Legacy Waste Disposal

* Out of 255 lakh MT of legacy waste across 164
dumpsites, 253.62 lakh MT has been scientifically
processed and disposed of.

+  Thestate government is committed to preventing new

legacy waste by ensuring regular and efficient daily
waste management.

6.3 Construction and Demolition Waste
Management

MoEF&CC, Government of India, notified the Construction
and Demolition Waste Management Rules, 2016 on 29th
March 2016. These rules apply across Gujarat, covering all
Municipal Corporations, Municipalities, Gram Panchayats,
Census Towns, Notified Areas, Industrial SEZs, and
Townships.

As per the Rules, construction and demolition debris must
not be left unattended at project sites and must be
disposed of in compliance with prescribed guidelines.
ULBs are required to:

+ Develop designated sites for C&D waste processing and
final disposal.

+ Identify authorized contractors for C&D waste
management.

+  Providea toll-free helpline for reporting and managing
C&D waste.

+ Equip vehicles transporting C&D waste with GPS
tracking to ensure proper disposal.

C&D Waste Processing Facilities

* Gujarat currently has 5 processing sites with a
combined capacity of 1,300 MT/day for managing
construction and demolition waste.
6.4 Plastic Waste Management

The Plastic Waste Management Rules, 2016, along with
amendments in 2018 and 2022, provide a comprehensive
framework for managing plastic waste, including Extended
Producer Responsibility (EPR), phase-out of identified
single-use plastics, labeling requirements, and source
segregation. GPCB is effectively implementing these rules
across the state through enforcement, awareness, EPR
monitoring, and support for alternative solutions. GPCB
has facilitated registration of stakeholders, issued
compliance notices, promoted use of cloth bags, and
supported infrastructure like material recovery facilities
and reverse vending machines to ensure rule adherence.

Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) Leadership

Gujarat leads the nation in EPR compliance, having the
highest number of registered Producers, Importers, Brand
Owners (PIBOs) and Plastic Waste Processors (PWPs) onthe
CPCB portal:

+ Brand Owners: 136

+ — Producers: 820

¢ — Importers:2,558

. PWPs: 697

This reflects proactive participation by industries and
strong regulatory facilitation by GPCB to ensure
responsible collection, recycling, and disposal of plastic
waste.

Pilot Study for PWP Verification

To enhance transparency and compliance, GPCB assigned

the National Productivity Council (NPC) to conduct a pilot

study verifying operations of five registered PWPs.

+ Findings confirmed valid consents, effective pollution
controlsystems, and high recycling efficiency.

* Recommendations included improved sourcing

practices, infrastructure upgrades, and accurate
conversion factor usage.

+ These recommendations were later on adopted by
CPCB to strengthen EPR implementation nationwide.
Promotion of Sustainable Alternatives

GPCB has actively promoted eco-friendly solutions to
reduce single-use plastic consumption:

+ 30 cloth bag vending machines installed at major
temples across Ahmedabad, Surat, Ambaji, Dwarka,
Shamlaji, Salangpur, and ISKCON, distributing over
1,00,000 cloth bags.

+ 24 reverse vending machines installed statewide,

Medical care is essential for human life and well-being;
however, waste generated from medical activities can be
hazardous and infectious, posing risks of disease
transmission. It can foster pathogen growth and
contaminate non-hazardous waste, undermining solid
waste managementefforts.

To address these risks, the Government of India introduced
the Bio-Medical Waste Management Rules, 2016, under the
Environment (Protection) Act, 1986.

Rule - 4 specifies duty of occupier (generator) to take all
steps to ensure that such waste is handled without any
adverse effect to human health and the environment and
Rule -5 specifies the segregation in category and treatment
methods to be adopted.

As per the Rule - 10 of the BMW Rules - 2016, every occupier
of an institution generating, collecting, receiving, storing,
transporting, treating, disposing and/or handling bio-
medical waste in any other manner, shall make an
application in Form 1 to the prescribed authority for
authorization. Authorization is granted for a period of 5
years from the date of issue by SPCB.

Gap Analysis Study (Statewide Implementation)

+ As per CPCB guidelines, SPCBs must conduct a Gap
Analysis Study every 5 years to assess BMW
management practices.

+ GPCB assigned GEMI, Gandhinagar to conduct the
study covering all 33 districts of Gujarat.

+ Discrepancy in HCF registrations/memberships and
authorizations.

+ BMWcollection v/s generation gaps from HCFs.

+ — District-wise BMW generation v/s coverage of Common
Bio-Medical Waste Treatment Facilities (CBWTFs).

+ CBWTF route mapping and areas with coverage
beyond 75km.

+ Treatment capacity gaps in BMW management.
+ — Differences between bedded and non-bedded HCFs.

+ BMW generation v/s worst-case scenario based on
occupancy.

Outcomes and Future Planning

+ Findings assist SEAC and SEIAA in deciding on location
and capacity for new CBWTFs and granting
environmental clearances.

6.6 E-Waste Management

The E-waste (Management) Rules, 2022 are in force since
1st April, 2023. As per the Rules E-waste is defined as means
electrical and electronic equipment, including solar photo-
voltaic modules or panels or cells, whole or in part
discarded as waste as well as rejects from manufacturing,
refurbishment and repair processes.

This Rules apply to every manufacturer, producer
refurbisher, dismantler and recycler involved in
manufacture, sale, transfer, purchase, refurbishing,
dismantling, recycling and processing of e-waste or
electrical and electronic equipment listed in Schedule |,
including their components, consumables, parts and
spares.

For better management of E-waste, Extended Producer
Responsibility (EPR) is introduced to ensure
environmentally sound collection, recycling, and disposal
of end-of-life electronic products. It mandates producers to
channel E-waste through authorized recyclers, supports
circular economy practices, and helps minimize
environmental impact. As per the E-waste (Management)
Rules, 2022, all producers, manufacturers, recyclers, and
refurbishers are required to obtain registration on the
CPCB's EPR portal.

As per the EPR portal (https://eprewastecpcb.in/), there
are 579 registered Producers in thestate.

GUJARAT POLLUTION

Qe CONTROL BOARD
GPCB GANDHINAGAR
s

Capacity Verification Study of E-waste facility across the
state

To implement the Extended producer Responsibility
framework, GPCB entrusted the National Productivity
Council (NPC) to carry out a capacity verification study of 15
registered e-waste recycling and dismantling facilities
across the state. The study covered field visits, document
verification, and evaluation of infrastructure and
operational practices. The study found that the majority of
units are equipped with trained manpower, pollution
control systems, and follow environmentally sound
dismantling and recycling practices. While units generally
comply with GPCB and CPCB guidelines, the study noted
under utilization of capacity in several facilities, primarily
due to challenges in consistent e-waste procurement.
Positive practices observed included use of personal
protective equipment, well-marked storage areas,
maintenance of records, and implementation of pollution
control measures. NPC recommended further
enhancements such as adoption of Standard Operating
Procedures (SOPs), training of workers, installation of
acoustic enclosures for noise control, and implementation
of Energy Management Systems.

On average, 70% of material recovery occurs during
dismantling, while the remaining 30% is processed through
recycling machinery.Specific energy consumption varied
by facility size: 35 kWh/MT for large units, 25 kWh/MT for
medium units, and 17 kWh/MT for small units. Recycling
costs ranged between %5,000 and 210,000 per metric ton,
depending on scale and location. Despite general
compliance with GPCB and CPCB guidelines, several
facilities were found to be underutilized due to inconsistent
e-waste supply.

6.7 Battery Waste Management

The Batteries (Management and Handling) Rules, 2001
have been superseded by the Battery Waste Management
Rules, 2022, which came into effect on 22/08/2022 under
the provisions of the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986.
These new rules cover producers, dealers, consumers, and
entities involved in the collection, segregation,
transportation, refurbishment, and recycling of waste
batteries.

As per the rules, every refurbisher and recycler must obtain
one-time registration from SPCBs through a centralized
portal developed by the CPCB. They are also required to
submit quarterly returns by the end of the month following
each quarter.

6.8 Hazardous Waste Management

MoEF&CC, Government of India, has notified the Hazardous
and Other Wastes (Management and Transboundary
Movement) Rules, 2016 (HOWM Rules, 2016) to ensure
environmentally sound management of hazardous and
other wastes. As per the provisions of these rules,
hazardous waste generators are required to adopt a waste
management hierarchy that prioritizes prevention,
minimization, reuse, recycling, recovery, and
utilization—including co-processing—before considering
final disposal.SPCBs are empowered to grant
authorizations for handling hazardous wastes and play a
crucial role in ensuring compliance. In line with these
mandates, the Board is proactively implementing
measures for effective handling, treatment, and
environmentally sound disposal of hazardous wastes,
while also promoting resource recovery and sustainable
alternatives such as co-processing and pre-processing.

Co-Processing of Hazardous and Other Waste

Gujarat, being one of the most industrialized states, has a
significant presence of pharmaceutical, chemical, dye and
dye intermediates, pesticide, refining and petrochemical,
ceramic, textile, and automobile sectors. Naturally, waste
generation is proportional to the number of industries. To
ensure sustainable development, Gujarat has
simultaneously developed common environmental
infrastructure to safely dispose of industrial wastes.

Historically, hazardous waste from industries was disposed
of at Treatment, Storage, and Disposal Facilities (TSDFs),
through incineration, or via Multiple Effect Evaporators
(MEEs). Typically, inorganic waste was sent to TSDFs, while
high-calorific-value waste was incinerated. The first
approach consumes large land parcels, and the second
consumes fossil fuels.

Conventional disposal methods increase the demand for
natural resources, require extensive land, and contribute to
emissions such as greenhouse gases. In the long run, these
methods pose health hazards and risks of groundwater and
soil contamination. Above challenges is catered by
diverting waste into cement industries for co-processing.

Cement production involves heating raw material mixtures
at 1200-1400°C, during which hazardous and other wastes
are completely destroyed, and residues are fixed in the
clinker. Using alternative raw materials reduces the need
for quarrying and improves the overall environmental
footprint.

Cement manufacturing involves burning a prepared mix of
raw materials in a kiln and grinding the resulting “clinker”
with gypsum. The GPCB introduced the innovative idea of
using hazardous waste as an alternative raw material or
fuelin cement plants instead of conventional disposal.
Co-processing is the practice of introducing alternative
fuels and raw materials into cement production in place of
conventional resources. This approach:

+ Reduces dependence on fossil fuels.
+ Lowers atmospheric emissions.

+ Provides a sustainable method for hazardous waste
disposal.

GPCB has encouraged industrial associations and cement
plants to establish infrastructure for feeding systems, waste
storage, handling, and laboratory facilities. Over the years,
this concept has also been adopted by other states.

To further support this initiative, Waste Collection Centers
and pre-processing facilities were promoted to divert low-
calorific-value waste into co-processing.

Since 2009, Gujarat has co-processed 52.40 million MT of
waste in cement kilns, including 6.764 million MT (with
plastic waste) during 2024-25. Additionally, 1.005 million
MT of plastic waste has been co-processed in cement kilns
during the same period. Importantly, these substitutions
have no negative impact on production processes,
emissions, or the quality of the final product.

Pre-Processing- A Micro-initiative to boost up co-
processing

GPCB has pioneered the concept of pre-processing in India
to divert high-volume, high-calorific, and incinerable
wastes into cementindustries instead of incineration.

Not all waste types are directly suitable for co-processing in
cement kilns. Pre-processing involves treating different
waste streams to make them homogeneous and
technically compatible with cement production. This
ensures compliance with environmental standards and
stable kiln operation.

Some wastes can also be used as raw materials either
directly or after pre-processing. Low-calorific-value and
low-volume wastes are also encouraged for co-processing
after pre-treatment.

GUJARAT POLLUTION

CONTROL BOARD
GANDHINAGAR

Pre-processing requires specialized expertise and
investment in machinery, installations, laboratories,
shredders, separators, homogenizers, and dryers. It aligns
with the Hazardous and Other (Management &
Transboundary Movement) Rules, 2016, following the
waste managementhierarchy.

This practice contributes to the circular economy by:
+ — Reducing fossil fuel and raw material usage.

+ — Ensuring clean material cycles.

+ — Eliminating harmful substances.

With proper guidelines, pre-processing can significantly
reduce waste requiring disposal. Fractions such as SCF,
RDF, plastic and packaging waste, tire chips, biomass, agro-
waste, and non-recyclable materials can be converted into
Alternative Fuels and Raw Materials (AFR) for cementkilns.

Currently, Gujarat has 14 pre-processing facilities, and
approximately 5.124 lakh MT of waste was pre-processed
during 2024-25.

Advantages of Preprocessing

+ Enables diversion of low-calorific-value waste
unsuitable for direct use in cement kilns.

* Ensures uniform physical and chemical
characteristics of waste, preventing:

+ Unloading difficulties

+ — Storage and feeding issues

+ Process and product quality disturbances
+ — Emission fluctuations

+ Legal and safety compliance issues

+ Supports the goal of zero incineration and landfill in
Gujarat.

+ Converts large volumes of waste into uniform,
acceptable-quality fuel.

7.1 Air Quality Monitoring

The Board continued its efforts under the Ambient Air
Quality Monitoring (AAQM) Programme by conducting
regular monitoring of ambient air quality at key urban
centres and major GIDCs including Ahmedabad, Vadodara,
Surat, Rajkot, Ankleshwar, Vapi etc. Sampling is carried out
in accordance with prescribed standards, and the
monitored parameters included PM,o, PM>.5, SO2, NOW,
NH 3, O03, CO, Lead, Arsenic, Nickel, Benzene, and
Benzo(a)pyrene.

Ambient air quality monitoring is undertaken as part of
various initiatives such as the National Air Quality
Monitoring Programme (NAMP) and the State Air Quality
Monitoring Programme (SAMP) to strengthen air quality
monitoring efforts across the State.

7.1.1 National Air Quality Monitoring
Programme (NAMP)

NAMP is a nation-wide programme of ambient air quality
monitoring. The objectives of the NAMP are to determine
status and trends of ambient air quality; to ascertain
whether the prescribed ambient air quality standards are
violated; to Identify Non-attainment Cities; to obtain the
knowledge and understanding for developing preventive
and corrective measures, to understand the nature's self-
cleansing process including dilution, dispersion, wind-
based movement, dry deposition, precipitation and
chemical transformation of pollutants.

Ambient Air Quality Monitoring is conducted at 83 stations
across the State under this programme, with the help of the
CPCB, Delhi. The monitoring of pollutants is carried out for
24 hours with a frequency of twice a week, to have one
hundred and four (104) observations in a year. Annual
average of year 2024-25 is mentioned in Annexure - 16 for
allocations.

7.1.2 State Air Quality Monitoring
programme (SAMP)

SAMP is a state level programme of ambient air quality
monitoring. Ambient Air Quality Monitoring is conducted at
26 stations across the State under this programme.

7.1.3 Volatile organic compound (VOC)
Monitoring

GPCB initiated a dedicated Volatile Organic Compounds
(VOC) monitoring program across major industrial clusters
in the State covering 27 VOC monitoring locations. This
initiative aims to assess the presence and concentration of
VOCs in ambient air, which are known to have significant
environmental and health impacts.

VOC monitoring is conducted twice a month at each of the
27 locations, with continuous sampling carried out over a
24-hour period as per the guidelines prescribed by the
CPCB. The data generated helps in identifying pollution
trends, and formulating effective mitigation strategies.

Environmental Quality Monitoring Programmes

This program reflects GPCB's commitment to

and ensuring compliance with national environmental
standards. Annual average of year 2024-25 is mentioned in

7.1.4 Continuous Ambient Air Quality
Monitoring (CAAQMS)

To strengthen air quality monitoring across the state, GPCB
has installed five Continuous Ambient Air Quality
Monitoring Stations (CAAQMS) at strategic
locations—Maninagar, Vatva, Vapi, Ankleshwar, and
Gandhinagar. These stations continuously monitor key
ambient air pollutants and transmit real-time data to the
“Sameer” App, which is managed by the CPCB. The data is
also used to calculate Air Quality Index (AQI), thereby
ensuring greater transparency and public awareness.

To further expand and enhance the monitoring network,
GPCBis in the process of setting up five additional CAAQMS
at Vadodara, Surat, Rajkot, Mehsana, and Bhavnagar (one
in each city). These upcoming stations will be established

based on a data purchase model, enabling timely and cost-
effective air quality data. Installation is expected to be
completed in the next few months, thereby strengthening
the real-time air quality monitoring infrastructure across
Gujarat.

7.1.5 Real-Time Noise Monitoring Stations
(RTNMS)

To strengthen the efforts to mitigate noise pollution across
the state, the GPCB has established Real-Time Noise
Monitoring Stations (RTNMS) in four major cities of
Gujarat—Ahmedabad, Vadodara, Surat, and Rajkot. Each of
these cities has been equipped with four RTNMS units,
strategically installed to represent different noise-sensitive
zones: (I) Residential Zone, (ii) Commercial Zone, (iii)
Industrial Zone, (iv) Silent Zone.

Thus, a total of 16 Real-Time Noise Monitoring Stations have
been installed across the state. This initiative enables GPCB
to monitor trends. The data also supports the formulation
of effective policies and awareness campaigns aimed at
noise pollution control and public health protection.

GPCB has been actively involved in the systematic monitoring of water quality across the state through various Central and
State-sponsored programs. These efforts aim to assess the impact of anthropogenic activities on water bodies, identify
pollution sources, and support environmental planning and regulatory interventions. The following table presents the
number of water monitoring stations operated by GPCB

7.2.1 Global Environmental Monitoring
System (GEMS) Project

GPCB continues its proactive role in environmental
monitoring through its participation in the Global
Environmental Monitoring System (GEMS) project which is
an initiative of CPCB that has been active in Gujarat since
1980.

The project focuses on assessing water quality of major

rivers and groundwater sources across the state. GPCB

operates a total of 9 monitoring stations under the GEMS

project:

+ 7 Surface Water Stations: Monitoring water quality in
key rivers—Narmada, Sabarmati, Mahi and Tapi

+ 2Groundwater Stations: Monitoring borewell water in
Ahmedabad and Vadodara

Location wise annual average results of water monitoring
carried out under the GEMS project of year 2024-25 is given
in Annexure - 19.

Data from these stations is submitted to CPCB, New Delhi,
through the Environmental Data Bank (EDB) system,
ensuring timely reporting for national policy and pollution
controlefforts

Through the GEMS project, GPCB continues to provide
essential data to guide water quality management and
policy formulation at both state and national levels. The
bifurcation of surface and groundwater monitoring
ensures comprehensive surveillance of aquatic
ecosystems and drinking water sources, reinforcing
Gujarat's commitment to environmental protection.

7.2.3. Water quality under the Annual Status
Project

GPCB undertakes annual statewide water quality
assessments under the Annual Status Water Monitoring
Project. This initiative covers surface water (rivers, lakes,
reservoirs, creeks), groundwater (borewells, tubewells,
open wells), and selected urban water bodies to evaluate
ecological health and public safety.

Location wise annual average results of water monitoring
carried out under the Annual status project for the year
2024-25 is givenin Annexure - 21.

The Annual Water Quality Monitoring Project 2024-25
presents a comprehensive picture of aquatic ecosystem
health in Gujarat. GPCB will continue to enhance
surveillance and data transparency to support better
pollution control strategies, ecosystem restoration, and
public health protection.

7.2.6 Bio monitoring of water bodies

To assess the ecological health of Gujarat's water bodies,
GPCB undertakes biological monitoring. This program
complements chemical assessments by focusing on
aquatic life, particularly benthic macroinvertebrates, as
indicators of water quality over time. Biological monitoring /
offers a long-term, cost-effective, and integrated view of
water quality. Aquatic organisms reflect the cumulative
impact of pollutants, making them ideal for tracking
ecosystem health and supporting restoration planning.
Location wise results of water monitoring carried out under
the Bio monitoring project of year 2024-25 is given in

Annexure - 24.

Biological Water Quality Criteria (BWQC)

7.2.7 Coastal Monitoring

Gujarat, with India's longest coastline of 2,340.62 km,
borders the Arabian Sea and hosts major ports such as
Kandla, Mundra, Pipavav, Dahej and Hazira. This coastal
region supports extensive industrial, urban, and port-
based activities, which contribute to pollution through
direct discharge or via rivers draining into the sea.

To assess the environmental impact and identify pollution
hotspots, GPCB conducts coastal and estuarine water
quality monitoring at strategically selected stations. This
program is aligned with GPCB's commitment to
sustainable coastal development and marine ecosystem
protection. Details of the coastal water quality monitoring
stations, along with the annual average values of major
environmental parameters, are provided inthe

Annexure - 25.

Continued surveillance, along with effective industrial
effluent management and municipal sewage treatment, is
vitalto preserving Gujarat's coastal ecosystem.

7.3

In Gujarat, major festivals such as Navratri, Diwali, and
Ganesh Utsav are celebrated with great fervour. These
events, however, can have environmental
impacts—particularly on air quality, noise levels, and water
bodies—due to fireworks, loudspeakers, and idol
immersions.

Festival Monitoring

To address these concerns, GPCB conducts special
monitoring drives during these festivals, as per guidelines
from CPCB. The objectives are to assess pollution levels,
promote environmentally responsible celebration
practices, and ensure public awareness.

7.3.1 Ganesh Utsav (Water Quality
Monitoring due to Idol Immersion)

Ganesh idol immersions take place in natural water bodies
and artificial ponds prepared by municipal authorities.
GPCB monitors water quality at 106 locations across
Gujarat in three phases:

. Pre-Immersion
. During Immersion

. Post-Immersion

Key parameters monitored include pH, Dissolved Oxygen
(DO), Biological Oxygen Demand (BOD), and Chemical
Oxygen Demand (COD). Regional office wise water sample
analysis summary of year 2024-25 is given in Annexure 26.

7.3.2 Noise Monitoring During Navratri

Navratri is one of the most widely celebrated festivals in
Gujarat, marked by large public gatherings, music, and
cultural programs, especially late into the night. In view of
the potential impact on ambient noise levels during this
period, the GPCB undertakes special noise monitoring
activities every year.

For 2024-25, a total of 436 noise samples were collected
from various locations during the Navratri festival. Regional
office wise noise level analysis summary of year 2024-25 is
given in Annexure 27. Also, graphical presentation is given
below for RO wise noise levels measured during Navratri.
Additionally, special drives were conducted in Ahmedabad
and Vadodara through third-party agencies by GPCB at
different locations of party plots to monitor the noise level
in Navratri festival. Results ofthe same is as below.

CHAPTER: VIII
Other Details

8.1 Water Cess

The Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Cess Act,
1977 provides for the levy and collection of cess from
industries and local authorities based on their water
consumption. To augment the resources of the Central and
State Pollution Control Boards, the Government of India
formulated necessary rules under this Act, which were
notified on 26th July 1978 under Section 17.

GPCB has been designated as the authority for collecting
water cess from industries and local bodies in the state.
Under Section 6 of the Act, the power to levy and recover
water cess has been delegated to the Member Secretary of
the Board, while the authority to remit collections to the
Central Government under Section 6(4) has been conferred
to the Board by the State Government. As per the rules,
MoEF&CC remits up to 80% of the total cess collected to the
respective State Board.

As part of GPCB's E-Governance initiative, the Online XGN
program was introduced to facilitate applicants in filing
their Water Cess Returns online under the Act. From 1st
September 2009, online filing became mandatory, and
from June 2016, e-payment was made compulsory for cess
payments.

The Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Cess Act,
1977 was fully repealed through a Central Government
Notification dated 05/05/2017 under The Taxation Laws
(Amendment) Act, 2017, and water cess was subsequently
subsumed into GST from 1st July 2017. Thereafter, cess
collection is only applicable for industries with pending
dues up to 30/06/2017.

The Status regarding the assessment of Water Cess collection is as below:

Particulars

2021-22 2022-23 2023-24 2024-25
1 Number of Unit assessed 480 1476 763 5967
2 Number of assessment order issued 1128 4598 2045 31876
3 Amount collected Rs. In lakh 585.85 140.87 152.65 161.86

8.2. Comprehensive Environmental
Pollution Index (CEPI)

CPCB has developed a Comprehensive Environmental
Pollution Index (CEPI). The Hon'ble National Green
Tribunal (NGT), Principal Bench, New Delhi, issued orders
in O.A. No. 1038/2018 dated 10/07/2019, 23/08/2019, and
14/11/2019, stating that:

+ Areas with a CEPI score above 70 are classified as
Critically Polluted Areas (CPA).

+ Areas witha CEPI score between 60-70 are classified as
Severely Polluted Areas (SPA).

+ Areas witha CEPI score below 60 are classified as Other
Polluted Areas (OPA).

As per the Hon'ble NGT order dated 10/07/2019, in the
matter of O.A. No. 1038/2018, the Polluted Industrial Areas
(PIAs) of Gujarat, based on the CEPI-2016 protocol, are
identified as follows:

The Hon'ble NGT, in its Order dated 10/07/2019 (0.A. No.
1038/2018), directed that no new industrial activities or
expansion of Red and Orange category units be allowed in
critically and severely polluted areas (CPAs/SPAs) until
environmental parameters improve or carrying capacity is
assessed. The order also instructed the assessment and
recovery of compensation for the last 5 years, with interim
compensation set at 21 crore for large industries, 750 lakh
for medium, and 225 lakh for small units (as per O.A. No.
95/2018 for Vapi).

To comply Hon. NGT order, CPCB prepared a mechanism for
environmental management of CPAs/SPAs and circulated it
to SPCBs/PCCs on 25/10/2019. Subsequently, GPCB issued
an Office Order on 11/11/2019 for managing CPAs/SPAs and
evaluating projects in these areas. A state committee,
chaired by the Additional Chief Secretary, was formed to
finalize action plans for Gujarat's CPAs/SPAs. The final
action plan and CEPI scores were submitted to CPCB on
16/01/2021.

The Gujarat Chamber of Commerce and Industry
challenged these directives in the Supreme Court, which
stayed the NGT orders on 18/03/2020. On 25/02/2022, the
Court disposed of related civil appeals, continuing the stay
for eight weeks. Following this, MoEF&CC, via OM dated
05/07/2022, lifted the abeyance on previous directions.

Based on this OM, GPCB may impose additional conditions
in CTE/CTO for Red/Orange category projects in CPAs/SPAs
as per its 2019 mechanism. CPCB has also developed an
online CEP! portal, where states must regularly upload
Polluted Industrial Area (PIA) data for review.

8.3. Right to Information (RTI)

The Right to Information (RTI) Act, 2005 enables all citizens
to use the fundamental right to access information from
public authorities. GPCB has established a dedicated RTI
Cell at its Head Office to manage applications under this
Act.

In accordance with the directions of the Government of
Gujarat, GPCB has designated a Senior Environmental
Engineer at the Head Office as the Appellate Authority.
Additionally, an Environmental Engineer at the Head Office

and Regional Officers in 27 District Regional Offices have
been appointed as Public Information Officers (PIOs) to
provide requested information under the RTI Act.

During the year 2024-25, a total of 1,499 RTI applications
were received among that 561 were received at the Head
Office and 938 at Regional Offices. Necessary information
was provided to all applicants, and 177 appeal applications
were also received during this period.

8.4 Public Consultation as per EIA
Notification

As per Notification No. S.0. 1533 (E) dated 14/09/2006
issued by the MoEF&CC, Government of India, the
establishment of new projects or activities, as well as the
expansion or modernization of existing ones listed in the
notification's schedule, requires Environmental Clearance
(EC).

All Category 'A' projects and Category 'B1' projects or
activities must undergo Public Consultation, with certain
exemptions as specified. The authorities responsible for
granting EC under these regulations are the MoEF&CC and
the State Environment Impact Assessment Authority
(SEIAA), Gujarat.

8.5 Analytical Quality Control

The Central Laboratory, Gandhinagar participated in a
Proficiency Testing (PT) program organized by the NABL
approved PT provider M/s. Crystal Quality, Surat, for
chemical parameters during the year 2024-2025.

The Vadodara and Vapi Laboratories participated in a PT
program organized by NABL approved M/s. Horizon
Analytical Laboratory Private Limited, Pune, for
wastewater parameters during the same period. Similarly,
the Bharuch and Rajkot Laboratories took part in a PT
program conducted by NABL approved M/s. Green
Economy Initiatives Private Limited, focusing on
wastewater parameters for the year 2024-2025.

All analyses were carried out accurately and within the
required timeframe. The results were submitted to the
respective NABL approved PT providers and were found to
be satisfactory.

The Central Laboratory also conducted an Inter-Laboratory
Comparison (ILC) program with the Regional Laboratories
of Vadodara, Bharuch, Surat, Vapi, Rajkot, Jamnagar,
Bhavnagar, Kutch (West), Geer Foundation, Gandhinagar,
and other EPA and NABL accredited laboratories during
2024-2025, with satisfactory results. Additionally, the
Vadodara and Rajkot Laboratories conducted ILC programs
with four other NABL accredited laboratories, also yielding
satisfactory results.

These programs have contributed to continuous
performance improvement, morale boosting, and
enhancing the confidence of scientific staff, ensuring that
the analytical results are more precise and reliable.

Calibration of all essential scientific instruments is
regularly carried out by NABL accredited facilities in
accordance with ISO/IEC 17025:2017 procedures. NIST-
traceable Certified Reference Materials (CRM) are used for
intermediate checks during testing activities.

8.6 International Organization for
Standardization

The GPCB Head Office, Gandhinagar, along with its regional
offices, has successfully obtained 1IS/ISO 9001:2015 and
IS/ISO 14001:2015 certifications for 25 out of 27 Regional
Offices in the state. These certifications aim to ensure
uniformity and consistency in procedures, continuous
performance improvement, and enhanced environmental
protection across all operations.

Additionally, the Head Office and the Regional Offices of
Vadodara, Bharuch, Vapi, Surat, Bhavnagar, Rajkot, and
Kutch (West) are certified forthe following:

* Quality Management System (QMS) - 1S/ISO 9001:2015

+ Environmental Management System (EMS) - IS/ISO
14001:2015

* Occupational Health and Safety Management System
(OHSMS) -1S/ISO 45001:2018

The Board has developed a comprehensive Integrated
Management System (IMS) manual covering these
standards. Implementing these standards enables GPCB to
deliver better services to stakeholders while maintaining
high environmental and safety standards.

Furthermore, adopting the Occupational Health and Safety
Management System enhances workplace safety and
overall well-being for employees. To support effective
implementation, regular training programs are conducted
for GPCB personnelacross all offices.

CHAPTER: IX

9.1 Environmental Awareness Programmes at GPCB

Flag Off Ceremony of AMABAJI Padyatra - Swachh Paryavaran Yatra

a

WORLD EARTH DAY 2024
Date: April 22, 2024

GPCB commemorated World Earth Day 2024 through a
series of collaborative programs across its regional offices.
In alignment with the global theme “Planet Vs. Plastics”, the
initiatives aimed to raise awareness about plastic pollution
and promote sustainable environmental practices.

Regional Office - Anand

In partnership with the Khambhat Industrial Association,
the Regional Office - Anand organized an awareness
program that drew participation from around 70 industrial
representatives and officials. A thematic lecture was
delivered by Ms. Disha Chhuchhar, Assistant Environmental
Engineer, emphasizing the global plastic crisis and the
importance of individual and industrial responsibility.

As part of GPCB's Cleanliness Fortnight 2023, participating
units were felicitated with certificates and awards. A key
highlight of the event was the announcement by Mr.
Bharatbhai Shah, proprietor of M/s Technichem Industries
Ltd., to adopt Lunej village with the goal of achieving a
plastic-free status. The initiative will focus on awareness
generation, waste segregation, and proper disposal of
plastic waste. Additionally, M/s Unity Dyechem Industries

/

expressed willingness to undertake a similar initiative in
Sokhada village. The Regional Office extended full support
forthese efforts.

Regional Office - Gandhinagar

The Gandhinagar office, in collaboration with M/s R-
Planet Integrated Management Pvt. Ltd., conducted a
knowledge-sharing event focusing on plastic waste
management. Mr. Rahul Patel, Assistant
Environmental Engineer, delivered a presentation on
“Beat Plastic Pollution - Need of the Earth”,
underscoring the urgency of addressing plastic
pollution. Regional Officer Mr. SV. Malsattar guided
attendees on compliance with relevant plastic waste
regulations and encouraged industries to obtain
required registrations.

M/s R-Planet representatives showcased their
recycling operations, offering insights into circular
waste management. The event concluded with a
hands-on plastic waste collection campaign at Zak
Industrial Area, where the collected waste was
directed to recycling facilities.
Regional Office - Junagadh

In association with Udankhatola Aerial Ropeway, operated
by Usha Breco Ltd., the Junagadh office organized an
awareness event at Mount Girnar. Mr. H.S. Baraiya,
Scientific Officer, addressed participants on the
importance of Earth Day. The event included the
distribution of jute bags bearing “Mission LiFE” messages
to ropeway staff and pilgrims at the check post in the
Bhavnath area, encouraging alternatives to single-use
plastic products.

Regional Office - Mehsana

The Regional Office - Mahesana collaborated with M/s Steel
Strip Wheels Ltd. to observe Earth Day through a
sensitization and plantation program. The event featured
addresses by Regional Officer Mr. D.M. Rathod and Mr. C.K.
Bavarva, highlighting the environmental hazards of plastic
pollution. A tree plantation drive was undertaken on the
company's premises, with active participation from both
GPCB and industry personnel.

Regional Office - Rajkot

A joint Earth Day celebration was conducted by the Rajkot
office and M/s Rajoo Engineers Pvt. Ltd. at their Veraval
(Shapar) facility. The event featured an address by Mr. H.A.
Rathod, Senior Scientific Officer, who elaborated on the
significance of reducing plastic use and adopting
sustainable practices in daily life. The session concluded
with a plantation activity and a vote of thanks by company
representative Mr. Praveenbhai Dave. Around 45-50
employees took partin the initiative.

Regional Office - Surendranagar

To foster environmental consciousness among youth, the
Surendranagar office organized an awareness program at
Gitanjali Vidyalaya, Wadhwan, in collaboration with
Sursagar Dairy. The event included an elocution
competition on the effects of plastic pollution, with top
performers receiving mementoes. A motivational address
on plastic hazards was followed by a pledge by students to
avoid single-use plastics, instilling early behavioural
change.

Conclusion

The World Earth Day 2024 celebrations across GPCB's
regional offices served as a platform to engage industries,
communities, and students in the collective mission to

GANDHINAGAR

GPCB

combat plastic pollution. These initiatives reinforced the
Board's commitment to sustainable development,
environmental awareness, and collaborative action in line
with state and national environmental objectives.

Environmental Awareness, Public Participation and Training

WORLD ENVIRONMENT DAY 2024
Date: June 05, 2024

Regional Office - Ahmedabad (East)

In celebration of World Environment Day on June 5, 2024,
the Regional Office of GPCB Ahmedabad (East) organized
impactful events under the theme "Land Restoration,
Desertification, and Drought Resilience." A tree plantation
drive was carried out with participation from all staff of the
Regional Office, Vatva, and nearby GIDC entrepreneurs. The
program commenced with the ceremonial planting of a
tree by Regional Officer Dr. Tallika Patel, who also delivered
apresentation on the importance of land restoration.

Her address, centered on the sub-theme "Our Land, Our
Future," emphasized the urgent need for collective
responsibility in environmental preservation and its direct
connection to public health and economic stability.
Through interactive dialogue with industrial stakeholders,
she advocated for proactive environmental action.
Approximately 50 saplings were planted at the Regional
Office, GIDC Phase-4, Vatva. Participants concluded the
event with a pledge: "Vatva Banshe Vrundavan,' reinforcing
their commitmentto a plastic-free Vatva GIDC.

Celebration at Naroda GIDC

A similar program was held at Naroda GIDC under a joint
initiative of the Naroda Industrial Association and GIDC.
GPCB officials were present as a tree plantation drive
unfolded, led by the President of the Naroda Industrial
Association. Mr. R.M. Ramanuj, Scientific Officer,
Ahmedabad (East), delivered an insightful talk on this
year's theme, reinforcing the message of sustainable land
use and conservation.

Through engaging discussions, he urged both business
leaders and citizens to take shared ownership of
environmental wellbeing. Around 100 trees were planted
near ITI, GIDC Phase-4, Naroda. Participants pledged
"Naroda Banshe Nandanvan," committing to a plastic-free
industrial estate. Mr. Ajay Patel, Secretary of the Naroda
Industrial Association, announced a year-long green
initiative with a target of planting 11,000 trees across
Naroda GIDC. The plantation site was officially named
"Akash Ganga Upvan."
Regional Office - Ahmedabad (Rural)

In alignment with the 2024 World Environment Day theme,
the Ahmedabad (Rural) office of GPCB, in association with
the Sanand Industries Association, organized an event at
Sanand GIDC to promote sustainable practices and
industrial collaboration. The event was graced by Hon'ble
Shri Kanubhai (MLA-Sanand), Shri Natubhai Patel
(President, Chamber of Commerce), and other dignitaries.

Regional Officer delivered a keynote address, highlighting
the critical role of industries in advancing environmental
stewardship. A significant highlight was the collective
pledge to plant and maintain 10,000 trees within Sanand
GIDC. This was followed by a tree plantation drive at several
industrial premises with strong participation from industry
partners.

Key contributors included Dana Industries, Nestlé India
Ltd., Hindustan Coca-Cola Beverages Pvt. Ltd., and ReNew
Photovoltaics Pvt. Ltd. At Hindustan Coca-Cola Beverages
Pvt. Ltd., a quiz competition was conducted and winners
were awarded cloth bags to encourage the use of plastic
alternatives. At Honda Motorcycle & Scooter India Pvt. Ltd.,
a drawing competition was held with prizes distributed to
winners. All the activities underscored the collaborative
effort required to restore the land and reduce
environmental degradation.

Regional Office - Bhavnagar

On World Environment Day, GPCB Bhavnagar organized
tree plantation activities across various industrial units. A
major highlight was the beach cleaning drive at Alang,
conducted in collaboration with the Ship Recycling
Industries Association and Gujarat Maritime Board.
Shipbreaking yard workers and officials actively
participated, reflecting strong community involvement.

Regional Office - Bharuch

The Bharuch office of GPCB marked World Environment
Day 2024 with a series of awareness and plantation
programs in partnership with the Dahej Industries
Association, Social Forestry Department, and Dahej Eco-
Friendly Society. Events included tree plantation, expert
talks, and recognition of winners from the Swachhta
Pakhwada competition.

On June 4, a riverbank cleanup at Nilkanth Mahadev
Temple, Bharuch, involved 125 volunteers from local
cycling and running clubs, the legal fraternity, and forest

Regional Office - Kutch (East and West)

World Environment Day 2024 through a beach cleaning
program at Kashivishwanath Beach, Mandvi. The event
began at 7:00 AM and witnessed participation from
approximately 150 industry representatives across the
Kutch district. Around 50 kg of waste was collected during
the drive.

The program concluded with the distribution of tree
saplings to all participants, promoting the message of
restoration and green commitment aligned with the theme
“OurLand, Our Future.”
